                                                       The Smallest String Attractors of Fibonacci, Period-Doubling, and
                                                                              Thue–Morse Words

                                                 Mutsunori Banbaraa , Hideo Bannaib,∗, Peaker Guob , Dominik Köpplc , Takuya Mienod , Yoshio Okamotod
                                                                                 a Graduate School of Informatics, Nagoya University, Nagoya, Japan
                                                                                 b M&D Data Science Center, Institute of Science Tokyo, Tokyo, Japan
                                                                       c Department of Computer Science and Engineering, University of Yamanashi, Kofu, Japan
                                                              d Graduate School of Informatics and Engineering, University of Electro-Communications, Chofu, Japan




arXiv:2602.16152v3 [math.CO] 12 Oct 2026
                                           Abstract
                                           A string attractor of a string T [1..|T |] is a set of positions Γ of T such that any nonempty substring w of T has an
                                           occurrence that crosses a position in Γ, i.e., there is a position i such that w = T [i..i + |w| − 1] and the intersection
                                           [i, i + |w| − 1] ∩ Γ is nonempty. The size of the smallest string attractor of Fibonacci words and period-doubling
                                           words of length at least 2 is known to be 2. The size of the smallest string attractor of the nth Thue–Morse word
                                           is known to be 4 for n ≥ 4. We completely characterize the set of all smallest string attractors of Fibonacci words,
                                           period-doubling words, and Thue–Morse words. For Fibonacci words, we show a recursive formula describing the
                                           2n−4 + 2⌈n/2⌉−2 distinct position pairs that are the smallest string attractors of the nth Fibonacci word for n ≥ 7. For
                                           period-doubling words, we show that the nth period-doubling word has exactly two smallest string attractors for every
                                           n ≥ 2, and give them explicitly. For Thue–Morse words, we show a formula describing the 32 sets of four positions
                                           that are the smallest string attractors of the nth Thue–Morse word for n ≥ 6. Our results show that strings with the
                                           same smallest attractor size can have a drastically different number of distinct smallest attractors.
                                           Keywords: String attractors, Fibonacci words, Period-doubling words, Thue–Morse words, Combinatorics on words



                                           1. Introduction
                                                For any string T , a set Γ of positions is a string attractor (or simply attractor) of T , if any nonempty substring of T
                                           has an occurrence that crosses (i.e., contains) a position in Γ. String attractors [1] are an important combinatorial no-
                                           tion that captures the repetitiveness (compressiveness) of the string in the sense that dictionary compression algorithms
                                           can be viewed as algorithms that compute string attractors. It is known that the size of the smallest string attractor
                                           of T , denoted by γ(T ), bounds all dictionary compression measures from below, and is NP-hard to compute [1].
                                                The study of string attractors on well-known families of strings has attracted much attention. Mantaci et al. [2, 3]
                                           initiated the study of string attractors on standard Sturmian words (which include the Fibonacci words), and Thue–
                                           Morse words. For any standard Sturmian word T , they showed that at least one of the sets Γ1 = {η + 1, η + 2} or
                                           Γ2 = {|T | − η − 3, |T | − η − 2} is a smallest string attractor of T , where η is the length of the longest proper palindromic
                                           prefix of T [1..|T | − 2]. For de Bruijn words of length n, they showed that the smallest attractor size asymptotically
                                           approaches n/ log n. They also showed an attractor of size Θ(log |Tn |) for the nth Thue–Morse word Tn . Later,
                                           Kutsukake et al. [4] showed that γ(Tn ) = 4 for all n ≥ 4. In a more general setting, Schaeffer and Shallit [5] discussed
                                           string attractors of all prefixes of automatic sequences, including the period-doubling words, Thue–Morse words, and
                                           Tribonacci words. In particular, they showed that the size of the smallest attractor of any prefix of the period-doubling
                                           word longer than one is 2, and the size of the smallest attractor of any prefix of length at least 25 of the Thue–Morse
                                           word is 4, using the Walnut theorem prover [6].

     For the Fibonacci words, defined as F0 = b, F1 = a, Fn = Fn−1 Fn−2 , it holds for n ≥ 3 that η = fn−1 − 2 (where
 fn−1 = |Fn−1 |), and the above result of Mantaci et al. translates to the sets Γ1 = { fn−1 − 1, fn−1 } or Γ2 = { fn−2 − 1, fn−2 }.
Since Γ2 cannot be an attractor (it can be shown that the length fn−1 suffix of Fn occurs uniquely and does not have
an occurrence crossing a position in Γ2 ), it follows that Γ1 is an attractor of Fn . Hence, this gives an explicit pair of
positions to be a smallest attractor of Fibonacci words. However, in general, a given string can have multiple smallest
string attractors. For example, any single position of a unary string is an attractor. For a more interesting example, the
set of all smallest string attractors of F8 is shown in Figure 1. See also Figure 16 for more examples.
     In this paper, we take a deeper look into the combinatorial aspects of string attractors of Fibonacci words, period-
doubling words, and Thue–Morse words, and give a complete characterization of their smallest string attractors. Our
characterization for Fibonacci words is based on occurrences of singular words [7] in the Fibonacci word. We show
a recursive formula describing the set of all 2n−4 + 2⌈n/2⌉−2 smallest string attractors of the nth Fibonacci word for all
n ≥ 7. For the nth period-doubling word Dn defined as D0 = a and Dn = ϕ(Dn−1 ) for the morphism ϕ(a) = ab and
ϕ(b) = aa, we show that the position pairs {3 · 2n−3 , 6 · 2n−3 } and {4 · 2n−3 , 6 · 2n−3 } characterize the smallest attractors
for n ≥ 3. For the nth Thue–Morse word defined as T0 = a and Tn = µ(Tn−1 ) for the morphism µ(a) = ab and
µ(b) = ba, we show the 32 sets of 4 positions that are the smallest attractors for n ≥ 6. Our proofs for period-doubling
and Thue–Morse words follow a common scheme: every smallest attractor of the nth word is shown to project, by
halving its positions, onto a smallest attractor of the (n − 1)st word, which reduces the characterization to excluding
a constant number of candidate positions by means of short substrings. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first
work which shows a complete characterization of the set of smallest string attractors for non-trivial families of strings.
     It is also worth noting that, although the smallest attractor size for both the Fibonacci and period-doubling words
is two, our results show that the number of smallest attractors can differ drastically, namely, from polynomial in the
length to constant. We do not yet have a clear interpretation of what this quantity captures, and its understanding
remains an open and interesting question, as it may allow us to consider more fine-grained types of repetitiveness.
Other related open problems are listed in Section 6.
     A preliminary version of this work appeared in the proceedings of CPM 2026 [8]. This version further adds the
full characterization of the smallest attractors of Thue–Morse words, which was announced in the presentation of the
conference version at CPM 2026 (June 2026), as well as details of the verification of our results using the Walnut
theorem prover [6].

Related Work
    Mieno et al. [9] gave a complete characterization of smallest straight-line programs (SLPs) of Fibonacci words,
showing that an SLP of a Fibonacci word is a smallest SLP if and only if it can be obtained by some implementation of
the RePair algorithm [10], i.e., the recursive pairing of a most frequent adjacent symbol pair. The number of distinct
smallest SLPs for Fn was shown to be 2⌊(n + 1)/2⌋ − 2 for n ≥ 3.
    We note that for the strings we have considered, the approach of Schaeffer and Shallit [5] and the software Wal-
nut [6] can be applied, and it is possible to compute a finite state automaton for symbols in ({0, 1}d )∗ , such that
accepting paths spell out a representation of the attractor positions (two for Fibonacci and period-doubling words,
and four for Thue–Morse words, i.e., d = 3 or d = 5) and the length of a prefix of the string. This approach is
discussed in Appendix A. While the exact sets of positions that the accepting paths of these automata represent are
not readily apparent from the automata themselves, by comparing them with automata constructed directly from our
characterizations, we have mechanically confirmed that our main theorems hold for all orders covered by their state-
ments (Appendix A.1). However, such a verification would not seem to give us much insight into why the statements
hold, which is what our combinatorial proofs provide. Schaeffer and Shallit [5] and Cassaigne et al. [11] have studied
another related attractor-based parameter span(w), defined as the smallest distance between the leftmost and rightmost
positions in an attractor of w.
    We recently became aware of the independent and concurrent work of Faro, Marino, and Pavone [12], which ob-
tains the same characterization of the smallest string attractors of Thue–Morse words as our Theorem 43 (Theorem 5.4
of [12]). Their proof is different from ours: it does not use induction on the order, but derives the characterization from
the sets of positions covered by the occurrences of the minimal unique substrings of three consecutive Thue–Morse
words (Lemma 5.1 of [12]), building on the characterization of these minimal unique substrings by Kishi et al. [13]
and on an exact description of their occurrences in the subsequent Thue–Morse words (Lemma 4.3 of [12]). Our proof

and 44).


2. Preliminaries

     Let Σ = {a, b} be a binary alphabet. An element of Σ is called a symbol. An element of Σ∗ is called a string. For
a string x, let |x| denote its length, and let ε denote the empty string, i.e., |ε| = 0. For any integer 1 ≤ i ≤ |x|, let x[i]
denote the ith symbol of x, and for any 1 ≤ i ≤ j ≤ |x|, let x[i.. j] = x[i] · · · x[ j], and x[i.. j) = x[i.. j − 1]. We also let
x[i.. j] = ε for any i > j. Let xR denote the reverse of x, i.e., if x = x[1]x[2] · · · x[|x|], then xR = x[|x|]x[|x| − 1] · · · x[1].
The complement of a symbol in Σ is denoted as a = b and b = a, and for any string x ∈ Σ∗ , its complement is
x = x[1] · · · x[|x|].
     For a set X of integers and another integer i, let i ⊕ X = X ⊕ i := {i + x : x ∈ X} and i ⊖ X := {i − x : x ∈ X}. For two
sets X and Y, let X ⊗ Y := {{x, y} : (x, y) ∈ X × Y}. Also, for a family X of sets and a set Y, let X ⊗ Y := {X ∪ {y} : X ∈
X, y ∈ Y}, and we write X1 ⊗ X2 ⊗ X3 for (X1 ⊗ X2 ) ⊗ X3 . For a string T and an occurrence w = T [i.. j] of a substring
w of T , we say that the occurrence of w crosses position k, if i ≤ k ≤ j. A boundary is a pair of consecutive positions
sometimes identified with two strings ending and starting at the respective positions. We say that an occurrence of a
string crosses the boundary if it crosses both positions of the boundary.
Definition 1 (String Attractors [1]). A string attractor, or an attractor for short, of a string T is a set Γ of positions of
T such that for any nonempty substring u of T , there exists an occurrence of u in T that crosses some position in Γ.
We denote by A(T ) the set of all smallest attractors of T .

    It is straightforward that string attractors are invariant under string reversal, meaning that the “mirrored” string
attractor is a string attractor of the mirrored string.
Observation 2. If Γ is an attractor of a string w, then (|w| + 1) ⊖ Γ is an attractor of wR .
     A string w is a minimal unique substring (MUS) [14] of T if w is a substring of T that has a unique occurrence in
T , and any proper substring of w has multiple occurrences in T . The following observation is straightforward.
Observation 3. For each MUS of T , any string attractor of T must contain at least one position that is crossed by the
unique occurrence of the MUS.

3. The Smallest Attractors of Fibonacci Words

3.1. Properties of Fibonacci Words and Singular Words
Definition 4 (Fn and fn ). For each n ≥ 0, the nth Fibonacci word is defined as F0 = b, F1 = a, and Fn = Fn−1 Fn−2
for each n ≥ 2. The nth Fibonacci number is defined as f0 = 1, f1 = 1, and fn = fn−1 + fn−2 for each n ≥ 2. Note that
 fn = |Fn |.
    Let F∞ denote the infinite Fibonacci word, of which Fn is a prefix for every n ≥ 1.
Definition 5 (Gn , ∆n ). For n ≥ 2, let Gn = Fn [1.. fn − 2] and Fn = Gn ∆n . For convenience, let ∆0 = ab and ∆1 = ba.
    Below are known or simple observations concerning Fibonacci words.
Observation 6 (e.g. [15, 16, 17]). The following propositions hold.
   1. For i ≥ 2, ∆i = ∆i mod 2 .
   2. For i ≥ 2, Gi is a palindrome (for i ≥ 5 by Gi = Gi−2 ∆i−2Gi−3 ∆i−3Gi−2 and induction; G2 = ε, G3 = a, and
      G4 = aba are palindromes).
   3. For i ≥ 3, Fi = Fi−1 Fi−2 = Fi−2Gi−1 ∆i .
   4. For i ≥ 5,

                           Fi = Gi ∆i = Fi−1Gi−2 ∆i = Gi−2 ∆i−2Gi−3 ∆i−3Gi−2 ∆i = Gi−2 (Fi−3 )R (Fi−2 )R ∆i
                              = Gi−2 (Fi−1 )R ∆i .

Definition 7 (Singular Words). Let S −1 = ε, S 0 = a, S 1 = b, and S n = S n−2 S n−3 S n−2 for n ≥ 2.
    Notice that |Fn | = |S n |, and all singular words are palindromes. In the literature [7, 18], these words have been
defined to be S n = Fn [ fn − 1]Fn [1.. fn − 1] = Fn [ fn − 1]Gn Fn [ fn − 1]. However, we start from the above definition
(Property 2 (4) in [7]) to simplify the presentation. The next lemma summarizes results shown in [7] that we utilize.

Proof. S n−3 occurs uniquely in Fn =           n−2

ized by Corollary 10) since it cannot occur in S n−2 (Lemma 8 (2)) nor cross the boundary of S n−3 S n−2 as well as
the boundary of S n−2 S (n−1) mod 2 (Lemma 8 (3); for the second boundary, note that S n−2 S (n−1) mod 2 is a prefix of
S n−2 S n−1 by Lemma 8 (1)). Similarly, S n−2 occurs uniquely in Fn , since no occurrence of S n−2 crosses the bound-
ary of S n−2 S (n−1) mod 2 . We have S n−3 = Fn [ fn−2 .. fn−1 − 1], S n−2 = Fn [ fn−1 .. fn − 1] from Corollary 10, and any
substring containing them are unique. Since Fn = Gn−1 ∆n−1 Fn−2 = Fn−2Gn−1 ∆n by Observation 6, it holds that
Fn [1.. fn−1 − 2] = Fn [ fn−2 + 1.. fn − 2] = Gn−1 and Fn [ fn−1 + 1.. fn ] = Fn−2 are repeating substrings. These are the
maximal substrings of Fn not containing S n−3 nor S n−2 . All substrings of these maximal substrings are repeating as
well, and include all proper substrings of S n−3 and S n−2 . Therefore S n−3 and S n−2 are MUSs. Also, since a substring
containing a MUS must be unique, there can be no other MUSs in Fn .
Definition 12 (Un and Vn ). For each n ≥ 5, define Un = [ fn−2 .. fn−1 − 1] and Vn = [ fn−1 .. fn − 1], i.e., the ranges of
positions crossed by the MUSs S n−3 and S n−2 , respectively.
Proposition 13 ([2, Thm. 5]). The size of the smallest string attractor of Fn is 2 for n ≥ 2.

    For any n ≥ 5, a Fibonacci word has exactly two MUSs and the ranges of positions crossed by their occurrence are
disjoint (Corollary 10, Lemma 11, Definition 12). Since an occurrence of a unique substring must contain an attractor
position (Observation 3) and from Proposition 13, the smallest attractor must consist of one position from each of the
two MUS ranges.
Observation 14. For n ≥ 5, A(Fn ) ⊆ Un ⊗ Vn .

    In Section 3.2, we first characterize a subset of position pairs in Un ⊗ Vn that cannot be an attractor of Fn by
arguments based on the occurrence of singular words in Fn . We then prove in Section 3.3 that all other position pairs
are attractors of Fn .

3.2. Invalid Attractor Position Pairs
    Here, we characterize position pairs in Un ⊗ Vn that cannot be attractors of Fn by using occurrences of singular
words in Fn . We first show that all occurrences of singular words S i in Fn can be captured by their occurrences in
our recursive definition (Definition 7). We interpret the recursion as a grammar rule, and show that each occurrence
of singular words in Fn corresponds to a node in the parse tree of this grammar, where a node in the parse tree
is considered to span exactly the interval of positions corresponding to the substring it derives. See Figure 2 for a
concrete example.

Lemma 15.    Consider the equations of Lemma 9 and Definition 7 as grammar rules that derive Fn for n ≥ 1, i.e.,
        Qn−2 
{Fn →     i=0 S i S (n−1) mod 2 , S −1 → ε, S 0 → a, S 1 → b} ∪ {S i → S i−2 S i−3 S i−2 | i = 2, . . . , n − 2}. Then, for any i ≥ 0,
every occurrence of S i in Fn corresponds to a node in the parse tree of this grammar.
Proof. Due to Corollary 10, there is no occurrence of S i in Fn before its first (leftmost) occurrence in a node of
the parse tree. Suppose there is a later occurrence of S i that does not correspond to a node in the parse tree of the

grammar, and consider the lowest common ancestor (LCA) in the parse tree of the leftmost position and rightmost
position of the occurrence. From the above observation, if the LCA is Fn , this implies that an occurrence of S i
crosses a boundary of S k S k+1 for some k ≥ i (or the boundary of S n−2 S (n−1) mod 2 , which is a prefix of S n−2 S n−1 by
Lemma 8 (1)), which violates Lemma 8 (3). Therefore, the LCA must be S k for some k > i. Furthermore, since
S i does not occur in S i+1 (Lemma 8 (2)), the LCA must be S k for some k > i + 1, thus, i ≤ k − 2. By definition,
S k = S k−2 · S k−3 · S k−2 . Therefore, S i must have an occurrence in S k (i) containing the center child S k−3 (only when
i = k − 2), (ii) crossing the boundary of S k−2 and S k−3 but not containing S k−3 , or (iii) crossing the boundary of S k−3
and S k−2 but not containing S k−3 . Case (i) cannot happen since S k−3 is not a substring of S k−2 (Lemma 8 (2)). Case
(iii) directly violates Lemma 8 (3) and Case (ii) also violates Lemma 8 (3) due to the fact that S i is a palindrome and
the statement of Lemma 8 (3) holds for S k S k−1 as well.
     In the parse tree of Fn based on the grammar defined in the statement of Lemma 15, we say that the occurrence of
S i is a center child of S i+3 if it is derived by the rule S i+3 → S i+1 S i S i+1 . Note that S 2 → S 0 S 0 has no (or an empty)
center child. We say a position p is crossed by a node in the parse tree if the occurrence of the string derived by the
node crosses p.
Lemma 16. Let n ≥ 5 and {u, v} ∈ Un ⊗ Vn . If u or v is crossed by a center child in the parse tree of some S i , then
{u, v} is not an attractor of Fn .
Proof. See Figure 3. Un and Vn correspond to the ranges of positions crossed by the MUSs S n−3 and S n−2 of Fn ,
respectively (Definition 12). Therefore, an attractor of size 2 of Fn must contain exactly one position from each of
the ranges. By definition, S n−3 = S n−5 S n−6 S n−5 and S n−2 = S n−4 S n−5 S n−4 . Here, S n−4 only occurs twice as children
of S n−2 , and does not occur elsewhere in the strings S n−3 and S n−2 (Lemma 15). Therefore, an attractor position must
be crossed by one of the two occurrences of S n−4 , and any position crossed by the center child S n−5 of S n−2 cannot be
chosen as an attractor position, since it would not be crossed by S n−4 . Similarly, there are three occurrences of S n−5 ;
two as children of S n−3 and one as a center child of S n−2 . However, since we cannot choose an attractor position in the
center child of S n−2 , an attractor position in S n−3 must be crossed by one of the two occurrences of S n−5 . Therefore,
a position crossed by the center child S n−6 of S n−3 cannot be chosen as an attractor position. The argument can be
repeated recursively. For j = 1, . . . , ⌈n/2⌉ − 2, given that the attractor positions must be crossed by occurrences of
S n−2 j−1 = S n−2 j−3 S n−2 j−4 S n−2 j−3 and S n−2 j = S n−2 j−2 S n−2 j−3 S n−2 j−2 that were not center children in the subtrees of
the parse trees of S n−3 and S n−2 , respectively, attractor positions in the respective center children S n−2 j−4 and S n−2 j−3
cannot be chosen.

Lemma 17. Let n ≥ 7 and {u, v} ∈ Un ⊗ Vn . If u ∈ Un \ {min Un , min Un + 1} and v ∈ Vn is in the right child of the
first occurrence of S n−2 , then {u, v} is not an attractor of Fn .
Proof. See Figure 4. Consider the occurrences of S n−4 (S n−3 [1..2]) in Fn . Due to Lemma 15, S n−4 has only three oc-
currences in S n−4 S n−3 S n−2 S (n−1) mod 2 , and thus in Fn ,two of which are the left and right child of S n−2 = S n−4 S n−5 S n−4 .
Since the last occurrence is only followed by a single symbol S (n−1) mod 2 , it follows that there can only be two occur-
rences of S n−4 (S n−3 [1..2]) in Fn . Therefore, for an attractor position to be crossed by S n−4 (S n−3 [1..2]), if one of the
first two positions in Un (corresponding to the first two positions of S n−3 ) is not chosen as an attractor position, then,
we cannot choose a position in the right child of S n−2 for the other attractor position. Here, notice that we require

n ≥ 7, since otherwise, the second occurrence of S n−4 (S n−3 [1..2]) crosses the boundary of the center and right children
of S n−2 .


3.3. Valid Attractor Position Pairs
    In this subsection, we show that the position pairs in Un ⊗ Vn that are not invalidated by Lemma 16 nor Lemma 17
are indeed attractors of Fn .
Definition 18 (Lk , Rk , and Lk′ ). For k ≥ 2, we define three subsets of positions within the first occurrence of S k ; let
uk be the node in the parse tree corresponding to this occurrence. Let Lk be the set of positions in the left child of uk
that are not crossed by any center child. Similarly, let Rk be the set of positions in the right child of uk that are not
crossed by any center child. Further, let Lk′ be the set of positions corresponding to the occurrence of S 2−(k mod 2) in
the leftmost path in the parse tree rooted at uk (uk itself when uk = S 2 ).
    For example, L6 = {21, 22, 24, 25}, R6 = {29, 30, 32, 33}, L6′ = {21, 22}. See Figure 2.

Observation 20. For k ≥ 2, min Lk = fk+1 , max Lk = 2 fk − 1, min Rk = fk+1 + fk−1 , and max Rk = fk+2 − 1.
    The following holds since all singular words are palindromes and thus the parse tree rooted at each singular word
is symmetric, implying that for any k ≥ 2, Lk and Rk are a “mirrored” image of each other with respect to some
midpoint.
Observation 21. For any k ≥ 2, Rk = ( fk+3 − 1) ⊖ Lk , Lk+2 = ( fk+4 − 1) ⊖ (Lk ∪ Rk ).

Lemma 25. For n ≥ 8, if (Ln−4 ∪ Rn−4 ) ⊗ Ln−3 ⊆ A(Fn−1 ), then Rn−3 ⊗ Ln−2 ⊆ A(Fn ).
Proof. See Figure 5. For any {q, p} ∈ (Ln−4 ∪ Rn−4 ) ⊗ Ln−3 ⊆ A(Fn−1 ) with q < p, let p′ = fn − p − 1 and q′ = fn − q − 1.
Since p ∈ Ln−3 and q ∈ Ln−4 ∪ Rn−4 , p′ = ( fn − 1) − p ∈ Rn−3 and q′ = ( fn − 1) − q ∈ Ln−2 by Observation 21. Thus,
{p′ , q′ } ∈ Rn−3 ⊗ Ln−2 . Since the mapping {p, q} 7→ {p′ , q′ } is bijective, it suffices to show that {p′ , q′ } ∈ A(Fn ).
     From Observation 6, Gn = Fn−1Gn−2 = Gn−2 (Fn−1 )R is a prefix of Fn . Essentially, p′ , q′ are positions in Fn that
correspond to the positions p, q in Fn−1 , mapped to the occurrence of (Fn−1 )R , i.e., p′ = |Gn | − p + 1 = fn − 2 − p + 1 =
fn − p − 1 and q′ = |Gn | − q + 1 = fn − 2 − q + 1 = fn − q − 1. Thus, by Observation 2, all substrings of (Fn−1 )R must
have an occurrence that crosses p′ or q′ . In order to prove that {p′ , q′ } is an attractor of Fn , consider any substring
u = Fn [i.. j] that does not cross p′ nor q′ . Notice that since p ≥ min Ln−3 = fn−2 , we have p′ ≤ fn − fn−2 − 1 = fn−1 − 1.
Also, since q ≤ max Rn−4 = fn−2 − 1, we have q′ ≥ fn − ( fn−2 − 1) − 1 = fn−1 .
        1. If j < p′ , then j ≤ fn−1 − 2, so u is a substring of Gn−1 , which is a substring of (Fn−1 )R .
        2. If p′ < i ≤ j < q′ , then u is trivially a substring of (Fn−1 )R .
        3. If q′ < i, then i ≥ fn−1 + 1 so u is a substring of Fn−2 , which is a substring of (Fn−1 )R because (Fn−1 )R =
           (∆n−1 )RGn−1 = (∆n−1 )R Fn−2Gn−3 .
In all these cases, u is a substring of (Fn−1 )R , which means u has an occurrence that crosses p′ or q′ . Therefore, {p′ , q′ }
is an attractor of Fn .

but not crossing q′ is a substring of Gn−1 , and has an occurrence crossing p′′ . Therefore, any substring will cross p′′
or q′ , and {p′′ , q′ } is an attractor of Fn .

4. The Smallest Attractors of Period-Doubling Words

Definition 29 (Dn ). For each n ≥ 0, the nth period-doubling word is defined as D0 = a, and Dn = ϕ(Dn−1 ) where ϕ is
a morphism defined by ϕ(a) = ab and ϕ(b) = aa. Note that |Dn | = 2n .
        Let D∞ denote the infinite period-doubling word, of which Dn is a prefix for every n ≥ 0.

Theorem 30. A(D2 ) = {{2, 3}, {2, 4}}. For n ≥ 3, A(Dn ) = {{pn , rn }, {qn , rn }}, where pn = 3 · 2n−3 , qn = 2n−1 , and
rn = 3 · 2n−2 .
     Schaeffer and Shallit [5] showed that for arbitrary prefixes of length at least 6 of the infinite period-doubling
sequence, {pn , rn } is a smallest attractor when the length is in [2n , 3 · 2n−1 ), and {qn , qn+1 }, when the length is in
[3 · 2n−1 , 2n+1 ). We consider only the length 2n prefixes, but characterize all smallest string attractors.

Observation 31. Since any occurrence of b in Dn must have come from an occurrence of a in Dn−1 by ϕ(a) = ab,
an occurrence of b in Dn is always at an even position. Hence, bb cannot occur. This implies that an occurrence of
aa starting at an odd position must be followed (provided it is not the end of the string) and preceded by ab, since it
could only have come from ϕ(aba).

    Furthermore, the above implies the following corollary.
Corollary 32. Any occurrence of a substring x in Dn with |x| ≥ 3 has the same parity, i.e., |{i mod 2 | Dn [i..i + |x|) =
x}| = 1.
Observation 33. The following are known or easily verifiable properties of period-doubling words.
(1) For n ≥ 1, (Dn−1 )2 = D′n c where D′n = Dn [1..2n ) and c = Dn [|Dn |].
(2) For n ≥ 2, Dn = Dn−1 (Dn−2 )2 = D′n−1 cD′n−1 c, where c = Dn−1 [|Dn−1 |].
(3) For any i such that 0 ≤ n − 2i < n, (Dn−2i )2 is a suffix of Dn , and for any 0 ≤ i < n, Di is a prefix of Dn .
    We first show that the two position pairs are indeed attractors of Dn .
Lemma 34. For n ≥ 3, {{pn , rn }, {qn , rn }} ⊆ A(Dn )

attractors of Dk−1 , Y ′ has an occurrence Dk−1 [i′′ .. j′′ ] = Y ′ crossing pk−1 (resp. qk−1 ) or rk−1 , i.e., i′′ ≤ sk−1 ≤ j′′ for

We consider the following O(k) cases (which together cover all lengths) depending on |X|.
   1. |X| > |Dk−3 D′k−2 |. See Figure 10 (a). Since the starting position of X is rk − |X| < rk − |Dk−3 D′k−2 | = 3 · 2k−2 −
      2k−3 − 2k−2 + 1 ≤ pk + 1, X crosses both pk and qk .
   2. |Dk−3 D′k−2 | ≥ |X| ≥ |(Dk−4 )2 |. See Figure 10 (a) and Figure 11. Since Dk−3 D′k−2 = Dk [pk + 1..rk − 1] = Dk [pk +
      1 − |Dk−3 |..rk − 1 − |Dk−3 |], it follows that Dk−3 Dk−3 D′k−2 has a period of |Dk−3 |. Since |X| ≥ |(Dk−4 )2 | > |D′k−3 |,
      X starts with a suffix of Dk−3 and therefore will have occurrences crossing pk and qk . More precisely, consider
      the occurrence Dk [rk − |X|..rk − 1] of X, which lies in Dk [2k−2 + 1..rk − 1] = Dk−3 Dk−3 D′k−2 . Shifting it to the
      left by |Dk−3 | = 2k−3 gives an occurrence crossing qk , which also crosses pk if |X| ≥ 2k−2 ; if |X| < 2k−2 , shifting
      it to the left by 2k−2 gives an occurrence crossing pk . In both cases, the shifted occurrences remain within
      Dk [2k−2 + 1..rk − 1], which has period 2k−3 .
   3. |(Dk−2i )2 | > |X| ≥ |Dk−2i | = |(Dk−2i−1 )2 | for i ≥ 2, k − 2i − 1 ≥ 0. See Figure 10 (b) and Figure 12. Due
      to Observation 33 (3), there is an occurrence of (Dk−2i )2 centered at (i.e., the first copy ending at) both r′ =
      rk − |(Dk−2i )| and rk . Since X is a suffix of Dk−2i D′k−2i and crosses r′ , it also has an occurrence crossing rk .
   4. |(Dk−2i−1 )2 | > |D′k−2i | ≥ |X| ≥ |Dk−2i−1 | = |(Dk−2i−2 )2 | for i ≥ 2, k − 2i − 2 ≥ 0. See Figure 10 (b) and
      (c), and Figure 12. X is a suffix of D′k−2i . There is an occurrence of (Dk−3 )2 centered at both pk and qk .
      Due to Observation 33 (3), there must be an occurrence of (Dk−2i−1 )2 also centered at pk and qk , and thus
      an occurrence of D′k−2i (Observation 33 (1)) of which X is a suffix. Since |X| ≥ |Dk−2i−1 | > |D′k−2i−1 |, X has
      occurrences that cross pk as well as qk .
   5. |X| = 1. D3 [p3 ] = D3 [q3 ] = a and D3 [r3 ] = b. It is easy to see that Dk [pk ] = Dk [qk ] = c and Dk [rk ] = c from
      the definition of ϕ, so X will cross both pk and qk , or rk .
Thus, {{pk , rk }, {qk , rk }} ⊆ A(Dk ), proving the lemma.
    Next, we will show that no other position pairs can be attractors of Dn with the help of the following lemma.
Lemma 35. For n ≥ 4, let {p, q} ∈ A(Dn ) and Dn [p] = a and Dn [q] = b. Then, p, q are both even and {p′ , q′ } =
{p/2, q/2} ∈ A(Dn−1 ).

Figure 12: Illustration of Cases 3–4 in the proof of Lemma 34 for i = 2 and i = 3. The highlighted substrings follow the same color pattern in (b)
and (c) of Figure 10.


Proof. The strings aaa, aab, baa, bab, abab, aabaa, aabab, babab, babaa, ababab are all substrings of Dn for
n ≥ 4. It is clear that q is even from Observation 31.
     We first claim that if p < 2n , then Dn [p + 1] , b. Suppose to the contrary that Dn [p + 1] = b. Then, for
aaa to have an occurrence crossing p, Dn [p − 2..p + 1] = aaab, where the underlined a corresponds to position p.
From Observation 31, we have Dn [q − 1..q] = ab, where the underlined b corresponds to position q. In order for the
substrings baa and bab to have an occurrence crossing one of these positions, Dn [q − 3..q + 4] = ababaaab, where
the bold a and ab are due to Observation 31. Next, in order for the substrings aabaa and aabab to have an occurrence
crossing p or q, Dn [p − 2..p + 3] = aaabaa and Dn [q − 5..q + 4] = aaababaaab. However, then, there cannot be an
occurrence of ababab that crosses p or q contradicting that they are an attractor.
     Next, we claim that p is even. If it is odd, then, from the above arguments, Dn [p..p + 3] = aaab. We also have
Dn [q − 1..q] = ab. For aab and abab to cross p or q, Dn [q − 3..q + 2] = aaabab. Furthermore, for aabaa to cross p
or q, Dn [p − 3..p + 3] = aabaaab. However, then at least one of babab and babaa cannot cross p or q.
     Finally, consider any substring X ′ of Dn−1 . If |X ′ | = 1, then, from the above arguments, Dn−1 [p′ ] = b since
Dn [p] = a could not have been derived by ϕ(a), and Dn−1 [q′ ] = a since Dn [q] = b must have been derived by ϕ(a).
Hence, X ′ crosses p′ or q′ . If |X ′ | ≥ 2, consider the corresponding substring X = ϕ(X ′ ) in Dn . Since {p, q} is an
attractor of Dn , X has an occurrence crossing p or q. Furthermore, since |X| ≥ 4 and the parity of its occurrences is
determined uniquely (Corollary 32), an occurrence of X at position k in Dn crossing p or q implies an occurrence of
X ′ at position ⌈k/2⌉ in Dn−1 crossing p′ or q′ .
Proof of Theorem 30. The cases n = 2 and n = 3 can be verified by brute force computation. For k ≥ 4, assume the
statement of Theorem 30 holds for all 3 ≤ n < k. Lemma 35 implies that any element other than {pk , rk } or {qk , rk }
in A(Dk ) would imply an element in A(Dk−1 ) not in {{pk−1 , rk−1 }, {qk−1 , rk−1 }} contradicting the induction hypothesis.
Together with Lemma 34, this completes the proof.


                                                                                     13
5. The Smallest Attractors of Thue–Morse Words

Definition 36 (Tn ). For each n ≥ 0, the nth Thue–Morse word is defined as T0 = a, and Tn = µ(Tn−1 ) where µ is a
morphism defined by µ(a) = ab and µ(b) = ba. Note that |Tn | = 2n .

    Let T∞ denote the infinite Thue–Morse word, of which Tn is a prefix for every n ≥ 0.
Observation 37 (Lemma 4 in [19]). For any substring w < {aba, bab, ab, ba, a, b} of Tn , the parities of all occur-
rences of w in Tn are the same.
Lemma 38 (Corollary 4.1.1 in [20]). Every substring of Tn longer than 2n−2 is unique.

Definition 39 (Bi,n , Bn , Ci,n , Cn ). For n ≥ 4 and 2 ≤ i ≤ 6, define u = i·2n−3 , Bi,n = {u, u+1} and Bn = B2,n ⊗B4,n ⊗B6,n .
Also, let Ci,n = {u − 1, u + 2} ∪ Bi,n and Cn = C2,n ⊗ C4,n ⊗ C6,n .
Remark 40. See fig. 13. In Tn for n ≥ 4,
    • B4,n corresponds to the boundary between Tn−1 and Tn−1 in the only occurrence of Tn−1 Tn−1 = Tn ;

    • B2,n corresponds to the boundary between Tn−2 and Tn−2 in the only occurrence of Tn−2 Tn−2 = Tn−1 ;

    • B6,n corresponds to the boundary between Tn−2 and Tn−2 in the only occurrence of Tn−2 Tn−2 = Tn−1 ;

    • B3,n and B5,n correspond to the boundaries between Tn−3 and Tn−3 in the only two occurrences of Tn−3 Tn−3 =
      Tn−2 .
The only occurrences of these substrings in Tn have been shown in [21, 22].

Lemma 41 (Variant of Theorem 2 in [4]). For n ≥ 4, (Bn ⊗ (B3,n ∪ B5,n )) ⊆ A(Tn ).
Proof. The size of the smallest string attractor of Tn for n ≥ 4 is known to be 4 (Theorem 3 in [4]). The proof of
Theorem 2 in [4] establishes that any substring of Tn of length at least 2 will have an occurrence in at least one of
Tn−1 Tn−1 , Tn−2 Tn−2 , Tn−2 Tn−2 , and Tn−3 Tn−3 , and crosses the boundary. Only {min B2,n , min B3,n , min B4,n , min B6,n }
is chosen for an attractor in [4]. However, since substrings aa and bb must cross—and therefore constitute—two of
the boundaries, any choice of selecting one position from each of the four boundaries will include positions crossed
by a and b, and thus will be an attractor for Tn . See fig. 13.

Lemma 42. For n ≥ 5, if A ∈ A(Tn ), then A′ = {⌈p/2⌉ : p ∈ A} ∈ A(Tn−1 ).

Proof. The strings abab and baba are substrings of Tn for n ≥ 4.
       We first claim that there exist positions p′a , p′b ∈ A′ such that Tn−1 [p′a ] = a and Tn−1 [p′b ] = b. Since Tn−1 [i] ∈ {a, b}
for any 1 ≤ i ≤ 2n−1 , it suffices to show that it cannot be that Tn−1 [p′ ] = a for all p′ ∈ A′ , or Tn−1 [p′ ] = b for all
p′ ∈ A′ . Suppose to the contrary that Tn−1 [p′ ] = a for all p′ ∈ A′ . Then, due to µ, it holds that Tn [2⌈p/2⌉ −
1..2⌈p/2⌉] = ab for any p ∈ A. Furthermore, since Tn−1 does not contain aaa as a substring and again due to µ,
the region surrounding the positions in A in Tn can only be {· · · baababba · · · , · · · baababba · · · , · · · baabba · · · } (or
possibly a substring of them, when p ∈ A is close to the start or end of Tn ), where the underlined ab corresponds to
Tn [2⌈p/2⌉ − 1..2⌈p/2⌉]. However, then, there can be no occurrence of baba crossing a position in A contradicting
that A ∈ A(Tn ). We can analogously rule out Tn−1 [p′ ] = b for all p′ ∈ A′ by considering occurrences of abab in
{· · · abbabaab · · · , · · · abbabaab · · · , · · · abbaab · · · }.
       Now, consider any substring X ′ of Tn−1 . If |X ′ | = 1, from the above arguments, there exist p′a , p′b ∈ A′ such that
X crosses p′a or p′b . If |X ′ | ≥ 2, consider substring X = µ(X ′ ) of Tn , which has an occurrence crossing some position
   ′

in A. Since |X| ≥ 4, by Observation 37, the parities of the occurrences of X are the same. Thus, an occurrence of X
at position i in Tn crossing a position in A implies an occurrence of X ′ at position ⌈i/2⌉ in Tn−1 crossing a position
in A′ = {⌈p/2⌉ : p ∈ A}. Hence, A′ is an attractor of Tn−1 . Since n ≥ 5, we have γ(Tn−1 ) = γ(Tn ) = 4 [4], and
|A′ | ≤ |A| = 4. Therefore, |A′ | = 4 and A′ ∈ A(Tn−1 ).

Theorem 43. For n ≥ 6, A(Tn ) = (Bn ⊗ (B3,n ∪ B5,n )).
    To show Theorem 43 we first show the following lemma.
Lemma 44. Let k ≥ 6 and A ∈ A(Tk ). If A ∈ Ck ⊗ (C3,k ∪ C5,k ), then A ∈ Bk ⊗ (B3,k ∪ B5,k ).
Proof. We show that there is no p ∈ A such that p ∈ i∈{2,3,4,5,6} (Ci,k \ Bi,k ) = i∈{2,3,4,5,6} {min Ci,k , max Ci,k }.
                                                           S                            S
    Due to Lemma 38, the substring Tk [1.. max C2,k − 1] occurs uniquely and thus max C2,k < A for any A ∈ A(Tk ) ∩
((Ck ⊗ (C3,k ∪ C5,k )) \ (Bk ⊗ (B3,k ∪ B5,k ))) since it cannot cross any element in Ci,k for i ∈ {3, 4, 5, 6}. Similarly
min C6,k < A for any A ∈ A(Tk ), due to the substring Tk [min C6,k + 1..|Tk |], since it cannot cross any element in
Ci,k for i ∈ {2, 3, 4, 5}. For any p ∈ i∈{2,3,4,5,6} {min Ci,k , max Ci,k } (except for max C2,k and min C6,k already ruled out
                                      S
above), we show that if p ∈ A, there exists a substring that does not cross any position in A, contradicting that A is a
string attractor for Tk , therefore implying p < A. We consider two cases depending on whether k is even or odd.
    For even k ≥ 6, we focus on the following four length-14 substrings flanking each Ci,k :
                           C4,k             C2,k             C6,k          C3,k or C5,k
                         z}|{             z}|{             z}|{              z}|{
                    bbaba abbaababb, aabab babaababb, bbaba ababbabaa, aabab baabbabaa.

The four (underlined) center symbols of each substring correspond respectively to C4,k , C2,k , C6,k , and (C3,k or C5,k ).
These are substrings respectively of T5 T5 , T4 T4 , T4 T4 , T3 T3 , which are in turn substrings respectively of Tk−1 Tk−1 ,
Tk−2 Tk−2 , Tk−2 Tk−2 , Tk−3 Tk−3 for any even k ≥ 6, where the center positions coincide, because Ti (resp. Ti ) is a
prefix and suffix of µ2 (Ti ) = Ti+2 (resp. µ2 (Ti ) = Ti+2 ) for any i ≥ 0. Since the lengths of the substrings we consider
                                                                   S
below are at most 6, any occurrence crossing an element in i∈{2,3,4,5,6} Ci,k must be a substring of the above strings.
Therefore, we can see:
    • min C3,k , min C5,k < A due to the substring aabbab;
    • max C3,k , max C5,k < A due to the substring babbaa;
                                                                   15
    • min C4,k < A due to the substring bbaaba;
    • max C4,k < A due to the substring abaabb;
    • min C2,k < A due to the substring abaa and the fact that min C4,k < A and min C6,k < A.
    • max C6,k < A due to the substring aaba and the fact that max C2,k < A and max C4,k < A.
See also Figure 14. This proves the lemma for even k ≥ 6.
   The case for odd k ≥ 7 is similar (albeit slightly simpler), due to the larger separation between the boundaries. We
focus on the following four length-22 substrings flanking each Ci,k :
                                            C
                                          4,k                        2,k    C
                                       z}|{                      z}|{
                              aabbaabab babaababbaabb, bbaabbaba abbaababbaabb,
                                         C6,k                  C3,k or C5,k
                                       z}|{                      z}|{
                              aabbaabab baabbabaabbaa, bbaabbaba ababbabaabbaa,

which have occurrences in which the four center symbols of each substring correspond respectively to C4,k , C2,k ,
C6,k , and (C3,k or C5,k ). These are substrings respectively of T6 T6 , T5 T5 , T5 T5 , T4 T4 , which are in turn substrings
respectively of Tk−1 Tk−1 , Tk−2 Tk−2 , Tk−2 Tk−2 , Tk−3 Tk−3 , for any odd k ≥ 7, where the center positions coincide. Since
                                                                                                              S
the lengths of the substrings we consider below are at most 10, any occurrence crossing an element in i∈{2,3,4,5,6} Ci,k
must be a substring of the above strings. Therefore, we can see:
    • min C2,k < A due to the substring bbaaba;
    • min C3,k , min C5,k < A due to the substring babbabaabb;
    • max C3,k , max C5,k < A due to the substring aabbabaaba;
    • min C4,k < A due to the substring abaababbaa;
    • max C4,k < A due to the substring bbaababbab;
    • max C6,k < A due to the substring babbaa.
See also Figure 15. This proves the lemma for odd k ≥ 7.


Proof of Theorem 43. The statement can be confirmed by brute force for n = 6. Let k ≥ 7 and assume the statement
of Theorem 43 holds for all n with 6 ≤ n < k. Lemma 42 implies that if there is an element not in (Ck ⊗(C3,k ∪C5,k )) but
in A(Tk ), then there is an element in A(Tk−1 ) not in (Bk−1 ⊗ (B3,k−1 ∪ B5,k−1 )) contradicting the induction hypothesis.
Therefore, A(Tk ) ⊆ (Ck ⊗(C3,k ∪C5,k )), and thus A(Tk ) ⊆ (Bk ⊗(B3,k ∪ B5,k )) by Lemma 44. Together with Lemma 41,
this completes the proof.

Corollary 45. For n ≥ 6, |A(Tn )| = 32.


6. Discussion

   While we studied the smallest attractors of Fibonacci, period-doubling, and Thue–Morse words of higher orders
(Fn for n ≥ 7, Dn for n ≥ 3, and Tn for n ≥ 6), for completeness, we list the smallest attractors of lower orders in
Figure 16.
   Some natural related open problems are:
    • Characterization of all smallest bidirectional macro schemes (BMS), or all smallest SLPs for the same family
      of strings considered. (Smallest SLPs for Fibonacci words are known [9].)

                                                           20
Appendix A. Using Walnut to Characterize the Smallest Attractors

   Using the approach by Schaeffer and Shallit [5], we can characterize the smallest attractors of period-doubling
and Fibonacci words using the following Walnut program. Below, we first introduce the connection, and then, in Ap-
pendix A.1, use the resulting automata to mechanically verify Theorems 23, 30 and 43 for all orders in their stated
ranges.

def pdfaceq "k+i>=j & A u,v (u>=i & u<=j & v+j=u+k) => PD[u]=PD[v]":
[strategy * CCLS] def pdsa2 "(i1<n) & (i2<n) & Ak,l (k<=l & l<n) => (Er,s r<=s & s<n &
 (s+k=r+l) & $pdfaceq(k,l,s) & ((r<=i1 & i1<=s) | (r<=i2 & i2<=s)))"::
def pdfa1 "An (n>=2) => Ei1,i2 $pdsa2(i1,i2,n)":
def pdfa2 "$pdsa2(i1,i2,n) & i1 < i2":

def fibfaceq "?msd_fib k+i>=j & A u,v (u>=i & u<=j & v+j=u+k) => F[u]=F[v]":
[strategy * CCLS] def fibsa2 "?msd_fib (i1<n) & (i2<n) & Ak,l (k<=l & l<n) =>
 (Er,s r<=s & s<n & (s+k=r+l) & $fibfaceq(k,l,s)
 & ((r<=i1 & i1<=s) | (r<=i2 & i2<=s)))"::
def fibfa1 "?msd_fib An (n>=2) => Ei1,i2 $fibsa2(i1,i2,n)":
def fibfa2 "?msd_fib $fibsa2(i1,i2,n) & i1 < i2":

    The code for the automaton pdfa2 for period-doubling words is taken from [5], except that we added the meta-
command [strategy * CCLS], which selects the determinization strategy CCLS [23] (see below). The code for
fibfa2 only changes PD (period-doubling words) to F (Fibonacci words), and specifies the numeration system the
corresponding automaton is defined for. Walnut returns TRUE for pdfa1 and fibfa1, i.e., every prefix of length at
least 2 of D∞ and of F∞ has an attractor of size 2 (for D∞ , this was shown in [5]); hence, pdfa2 and fibfa2 accept
exactly the smallest attractors of these prefixes.
    See Figures A.17 and A.19. While these automata characterize the set of all smallest string attractors of arbitrary
prefixes of the period-doubling words or Fibonacci words, their interpretation is not straightforward. To limit the
lengths of prefixes to the words we consider, we can modify the above to:
reg pow2 msd_2 "10*":
[strategy * CCLS] def pdfa2pow2 "(i1<n) & (i2<n) & (i1<i2) & $pow2(n) &
 Ak,l (k<=l & l<n) => (Er,s r<=s & s<n & (s+k=r+l) & $pdfaceq(k,l,s)
 & ((r<=i1 & i1<=s) | (r<=i2 & i2<=s)))"::

reg powfib msd_fib "10*":
[strategy * CCLS] def fibfa2powfib "?msd_fib (i1<n) & (i2<n) & (i1<i2) & $powfib(n) &
 Ak,l (k<=l & l<n) => (Er,s r<=s & s<n & (s+k=r+l) & $fibfaceq(k,l,s)
 & ((r<=i1 & i1<=s) | (r<=i2 & i2<=s)))"::
Figure A.18 shows the sub-automaton pdfa2pow2 of pdfa2 for paths corresponding to prefixes of D∞ of lengths
2k , and can be used to verify the results of Theorem 30. For Fibonacci words, Figure A.20 shows the automaton
fibfa2powfib, which is equivalent to the sub-automaton of fibfa2 containing only the accepting paths for prefixes
of length fk , which can be used to verify the results of Theorem 23.
     A similar program can be made for Thue–Morse words and 4 attractor positions:
def tmfaceq "k+i>=j & A u,v (u>=i & u<=j & v+j=u+k) => T[u]=T[v]":
[strategy * CCLS] def tmfa4 "(i1<n) & (i2<n) & (i3<n) & (i4<n) & (i1<i2)
 & (i2<i3) & (i3<i4) & Ak,l (k<=l & l<n) => (Er,s r<=s & s<n
  & (s+k=r+l) & $tmfaceq(k,l,s) & ((r<=i1 & i1<=s) | (r<=i2 & i2<=s)
  | (r <=i3 & i3<=s) | (r<=i4 & i4<=s)))"::
Here, the [strategy] metacommand is essential, as the computation was not able to finish under the default strategy.
The computation took around 6.5 hours on an Intel Xeon Platinum 8462Y+ processor (2.8GHz), using 705GiB of
                                                          21
memory. The resulting automaton tmfa4, analogous to pdfa2 and fibfa2, has 720 states (Figure A.21). We note
that the CCLS strategy also speeds up the computations for the period-doubling and Fibonacci words considerably:
with the metacommand [strategy * CCLS], the computations of pdsa2 and fibsa2 took less than one second and
about four seconds, respectively, whereas they took about 20 minutes and 2.5 hours with the default strategy. To limit
the lengths of prefixes to 2k , we add the restriction $pow2(n) as above:
[strategy * CCLS] def tmfa4pow2 "(i1<n) & (i2<n) & (i3<n) & (i4<n) & (i1<i2)
 & (i2<i3) & (i3<i4) & $pow2(n) & Ak,l (k<=l & l<n) => (Er,s r<=s & s<n
  & (s+k=r+l) & $tmfaceq(k,l,s) & ((r<=i1 & i1<=s) | (r<=i2 & i2<=s)
  | (r <=i3 & i3<=s) | (r<=i4 & i4<=s)))"::
The computation took roughly the same amount of time and memory as tmfa4. See Figure A.22.

Appendix A.1. Mechanical Verification of the Main Theorems
      Since the automata pdfa2pow2, fibfa2powfib, and tmfa4pow2 (Figures A.18, A.20 and A.22) accept exactly
the (representations of the) attractors of Dk , Fk , and Tk of the respective sizes, our theorems can be verified for all
orders in their stated ranges (k ≥ 3 for Dk , k ≥ 7 for Fk , and k ≥ 6 for Tk ) by showing that the sets described by our
theorems are regular, and that the corresponding automata accept the same languages.
      The automata read words over the alphabet {0, 1}d , where d = 3 for Dk and Fk , and d = 5 for Tk . For 1 ≤ j ≤ d,
the binary word formed by the jth components of the symbols of such a word is called its jth track. The first d − 1
tracks represent the attractor positions in increasing order, and the last track, the length track, represents the length of
the prefix (cf. Figures A.17 and A.19).
      For an integer x ≥ 0 and m ≥ 1, let JxKm denote the length-m binary representation of x (most significant digit first,
with leading zeros), which is used for Dk and Tk , and let ⟨x⟩m denote the length-m Zeckendorf representation of x,
which is used for Fk , i.e., ⟨x⟩m = dm−1 · · · d0 with x = j d j f j+1 and no two consecutive 1s. Recall that Walnut indexes
                                                          P
positions from 0, so a position p is represented by Jp − 1Km or ⟨p − 1⟩m , and an accepted word may be preceded by
an arbitrary number of copies of the symbol (0, . . . , 0) ∈ {0, 1}d . The length track is J2k Kk+1 = 10k for Dk and Tk , and
⟨ fk ⟩k = 10k−1 for Fk .

Period-doubling words. For k ≥ 3, Jpk − 1Kk+1 = 00101k−3 , Jqk − 1Kk+1 = 00111k−3 , and Jrk − 1Kk+1 = 01011k−3 .

Thue–Morse words. For k ≥ 6 and 2 ≤ i ≤ 6, the two positions i · 2k−3 and i · 2k−3 + 1 of Bi,k are represented by
Ji · 2k−3 − 1Kk+1 = Ji − 1K4 1k−3 and Ji · 2k−3 Kk+1 = JiK4 0k−3 .

Fibonacci words. For m ≥ 1, let am = ⟨ fm − 1⟩m , which is the length-m prefix of (01)∞ . Let X2 = {00}, X3 = {001},
and Xk = {0ak−1 } ∪ 0{0, 1}Xk−2 for k ≥ 4. Notice that every word in Xk starts with 0. For a set P of integers, let
⟨P⟩m = {⟨x⟩m : x ∈ P} and P ⊖ 1 = {x − 1 : x ∈ P}.
Lemma 46. For k ≥ 2, ⟨(Lk ∪ Rk ) ⊖ 1⟩k+1 = {ak+1 } ∪ 1Xk . Furthermore, for k ≥ 4,

                   ⟨Lk ⊖ 1⟩k+1 = {ak+1 } ∪ 100Xk−2 ,                     ⟨Rk ⊖ 1⟩k+1 = {10ak−1 } ∪ 101Xk−2 ,

and ⟨Lk′ ⊖ 1⟩k+1 = {ak+1 , 10k } if k is even and {ak+1 } if k is odd.
Proof. We proceed by induction on k: assuming the first statement for k − 2, we derive the representations of Lk ⊖ 1
and Rk ⊖ 1, whose union gives the first statement for k. The first statement holds for k = 2, 3 since (L2 ∪ R2 ) ⊖ 1 = {2, 3}
with ⟨{2, 3}⟩3 = {010, 100}, and (L3 ∪ R3 ) ⊖ 1 = {4, 6} with ⟨{4, 6}⟩4 = {0101, 1001}. Let k ≥ 4 and assume the first
statement holds for k − 2. Let u ∈ (Lk−2 ∪ Rk−2 ) ⊖ 1; by Observation 20, fk−1 − 1 ≤ u ≤ fk − 2, so ⟨u⟩k−1 is well
defined. By Equation (1), Lk ⊖ 1 = fk ⊕ ((Lk−2 ∪ Rk−2 ) ⊖ 1). If ⟨u⟩k−1 = ak−1 , i.e., u = fk−1 − 1, then fk + u = fk+1 − 1,
represented by ak+1 . Otherwise, ⟨u⟩k−1 = 1x for some x ∈ Xk−2 , i.e., u = fk−1 + u′ with ⟨u′ ⟩k−2 = x, and u′ < fk−2 since
x starts with 0. Then, fk + u = fk+1 + u′ is represented by 10⟨u′ ⟩k−1 = 100x. By the induction hypothesis, this gives
⟨Lk ⊖ 1⟩k+1 = {ak+1 } ∪ 100Xk−2 . By Equation (2), Rk ⊖ 1 = fk+1 ⊕ ((Lk−2 ∪ Rk−2 ) ⊖ 1), and since u < fk , ⟨ fk+1 + u⟩k+1 =
10⟨u⟩k−1 . This gives, again by the induction hypothesis, ⟨Rk ⊖ 1⟩k+1 = 10({ak−1 } ∪ 1Xk−2 ) = {10ak−1 } ∪ 101Xk−2 . The
union of the two representations is {ak+1 } ∪ 1({0ak−1 } ∪ 00Xk−2 ∪ 01Xk−2 ) = {ak+1 } ∪ 1Xk . Finally, by Equation (4),
Lk′ ⊖ 1 = { fk+1 − 1, fk+1 } if k is even and { fk+1 − 1} otherwise, represented by ak+1 and 10k .
                                                                22
    By Theorem 23, for k ≥ 7, the attractors {p, q} ∈ A(Fk ) with p < q correspond to the words whose tracks
(starting at the position of the leading 1 of ⟨ fk ⟩k ) are 00⟨p − 1⟩k−2 , 0⟨q − 1⟩k−1 , and 10k−1 , where by Lemma 46,
either ⟨p − 1⟩k−2 ∈ {ak−2 } ∪ 1Xk−3 and ⟨q − 1⟩k−1 ∈ {ak−1 } ∪ 100Xk−4 , or ⟨p − 1⟩k−2 ∈ {ak−2 } ∪ {10k−3 : k odd} and
⟨q − 1⟩k−1 ∈ {10ak−3 } ∪ 101Xk−4 .

Verification. All the sets above are regular: m {am } = (01)∗ (0 + ε) and k≥2 Xk = (0(0 + 1))∗ (0 010(10)∗ (1 + ε) +
                                               S                           S
00 + 001). Moreover, since all tracks are read synchronously from the position of the leading 1 of the length track,
whose position determines the order k, each family of sets in Theorems 23, 30 and 43 (for k ≥ 3, k ≥ 7, and k ≥ 6,
respectively) is recognized by a deterministic finite automaton obtained as a product of automata for the individual
tracks. We constructed these automata and checked, by a breadth-first search on the product automaton, that each
accepts exactly the same language as pdfa2pow2, fibfa2powfib, and tmfa4pow2, respectively, restricted to the
same ranges of orders (the restrictions of pdfa2, fibfa2, and tmfa4 to lengths 2k and fk also agree).
    Together with the remaining small orders, which can be checked by brute force (see also Figure 16), this gives an
independent, mechanical confirmation of Theorems 23, 30 and 43, relying only on the known sizes of the smallest
attractors of these words, and on the correctness of Walnut and of the (simple) automata constructions above. The
programs used for this verification (a self-contained one of a few hundred lines of Python, requiring only the standard
library, and a shorter one of about a hundred lines using the library automata-lib) were written with the assistance
of Claude Code (Anthropic) and are publicly available together with the Walnut commands and outputs at https:
//github.com/hdbn/allstringattractors.

Figure A.17: The automaton pdfa2 accepting a sequence s ∈ ({0, 1} × {0, 1} × {0, 1})∗ if and only if s[i] = (p[i], q[i], n[i]) where p, q, n are
respectively the binary representation of integers p − 1, q − 1, n, satisfying {p, q} ∈ A(D∞ [1..n]) and p < q. Here, the −1 is due to the difference in
the start index used for strings. By analyzing accepting paths for which n = 10k implying n = 2k we can verify the statement of Theorem 30. See
also Figure A.18.

accepting paths with n = 10k implying n = 2k . We can see that for k = 2, p = 001 implying p = 2 and q = 010 or 011 implying q = 3 or 4, i.e.,
A(Dk ) = {{2, 3}, {2, 4}}. For k ≥ 3, p = 00101k−3 or 00111k−3 implying p = 3 · 2k−3 = pk or 2k−1 = qk , and q = 01011k−3 implying q = 3 · 2k−2 = rk
(where pk , qk , rk are as in Theorem 30), i.e., A(Dk ) = {{3 · 2k−3 , 3 · 2k−2 }, {2k−1 , 3 · 2k−2 }}, verifying the statement of Theorem 30.

                                                                                                                                                                  (i1,i2,n): ?msd_fib $fibsa2(i1,i2,n) & i1 < i2




Figure A.19: The automaton fibfa2 accepting a sequence s ∈ ({0, 1} × {0, 1} × {0, 1})∗ if and only if s[i] = (p[i], q[i], n[i]) where p, q, n are
respectively the bit representation, in Fibonacci numeration (Zeckendorf bit representation), of integers p−1, q−1, n satisfying {p, q} ∈ A(F∞ [1..n])
and p < q. Here, the −1 is due to the difference in the start index used for strings. The accepting paths for which n = 10k implies n = fk+1 and
the values p and q implied from p and q should correspond to the statement of Theorem 23. See Figure A.20 for a simplified automaton containing
only such paths.

                                                   (i1,i2,n): ?msd_fib (i1<n) & (i2<n) & (i1<i2) & $powfib(n) & Ak,l (k<=l & l<n) =>
                                               (Er,s r<=s & s<n & (s+k=r+l) & $fibfaceq(k,l,s)& ((r<=i1 & i1<=s) | (r<=i2 & i2<=s)))




Figure A.20: The automaton fibfa2powfib, accepting the same language as the automaton obtained by removing, from the automaton in Fig-
ure A.19, all edges and nodes that are not part of accepting paths with n = 10k representing fk+1 in the Fibonacci numeration. Each accepting path
of length k + 1 starting with [0, 0, 1] represents a position pair in A(Fk+1 ), and the automaton can be used to verify Theorem 23 (see Appendix A.1).

Figure A.21: The automaton tmfa4 (720 states) accepting a sequence s ∈ ({0, 1}5 )∗ if and only if s[i] = (p1 [i], p2 [i], p3 [i], p4 [i], n[i]) where
p1 , p2 , p3 , p4 , n are respectively the binary representation of integers p1 − 1, p2 − 1, p3 − 1, p4 − 1, n, such that {p1 , p2 , p3 , p4 } is an attractor of
T∞ [1..n] and p1 < p2 < p3 < p4 . Here, the −1 is due to the difference in the start index used for strings. Since the automaton has 720 states, it
cannot be read from the figure, in which states are drawn as dots and edge labels are omitted; the automaton is available in machine-readable form
in the repository (see Appendix A.1). See Figure A.22 for the automaton tmfa4pow2 containing only the accepting paths for prefixes of length 2k .

                                        (i1,i2,i3,i4,n): (i1<n) & (i2<n) & (i3<n) & (i4<n) & (i1<i2) & (i2<i3) & (i3<i4) & $pow2(n) & Ak,l (k<=l & l<n) => (Er,s r<=s & s<n & (s+k=r+l) & $tmfaceq(k,l,s) & ((r<=i1 & i1<=s) | (r<=i2 & i2<=s) | (r <=i3 & i3<=s) | (r<=i4 & i4<=s)))




Figure A.22: The automaton tmfa4pow2 whose accepting paths correspond to 4 distinct positions that are attractors of the length 2k prefix of
the Thue–Morse word. It can be verified that there are 32 accepting paths that start with [0, 0, 0, 0, 1] for each length k + 1 ≥ 7 (corresponding to
n = 10k , i.e. 2k ), and the attractor positions correspond to those in the statement of Theorem 43 (see Appendix A.1). Since γ(Tk ) = 4 for k ≥ 4, the
accepted sets are exactly the smallest attractors for k ≥ 4; for k ≤ 3, they are attractors that are not smallest.
